The bishop is safe and puts pressure on g7/f8.
But Black can just move the knight or ignore it.

If I play Nd5 (c3d5):

1. Nd5 Nxg5
Now the bishop is gone, and the knight is on d5.

Wait:
White: Nd5, Bishop gone.
Black: Knight gone, Bishop gone.
This is just a trade.

But if 1. Bxf6, I force the trade now.
1. Bxf6 gxf6.
Now the bishop is gone, the knight is gone, and Black's pawns are ruined.

Which is better?
Bxf6 seems more concrete.
It removes the piece attacking the bishop and creates structural weaknesses.

Bishop is at g5.
Knight is at f6.
Move: g5f6.

Does Black have a mating attack?
Black Queen at f5, Rook at f8.
If White plays Bxf6, the f-file doesn't immediately become a threat because White has a rook at f1 and a king at g1. (Standard castling position).

Wait, what about Nd5?
1. Nd5
If 1... Nxd5, then 2. Bxd5. (Bishop on d5, Knight gone).
If 1... Qxd5, then 2. Bxf6 (No, the bishop is on g5). 2. Bxf6 is not possible.
If 1... Qxd5, white has simply lost a knight.
So Nd5 is only viable if Black cannot take the knight. But Black can.

So, g5f6 is the most logical move to resolve the tension on g5.

Wait, let's check for any other strong moves.
d1h5? (Queen to h5).
This puts the queen in an active position.
But if white plays d1h5, Black can play ... Nxg5.
Then White's bishop is gone for nothing.

So, the bishop on g5 MUST be addressed.
Either move it or trade it.
Bxf6 (g5f6) is a very common and strong move here.

Let's re-verify the position of the bishop and knight.
♜ ⭘ ♝ ⭘ ⭘ ♜ ♚ ⭘ (Row 0: r . b . . r k .)

Columns: a b c d e f g h
Col 6 is 'g'.
So Row 3, Col 6 is g5.
The piece there is ♗ (White Bishop).
Row 2, Col 5 is 'f6'.
The piece there is ♞ (Black Knight).
Yes, g5 is the Bishop, f6 is the Knight.
Move g5f6 is Bxf6.

Final check:
White: K g1, Q d1, R a1, R f1, B g5, N c3, Ps a2,b2,c2,f2,g2,h2.
Black: K g8, Q f5, R a8, R f8, B c8, B e7, N f6, Ps a7,b7,c7,e5,f7,g7,h7.
Bxf6 removes the defender of d5.
After 1. Bxf6, if 1... gxf6, white can now play 2. Nd5 comfortably.
